\displaystyle|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\tau,\mu^{2})=\frac{{\cal M}^{(-,-),(1)}_{gg\to ggg}}{{\cal M}_{gg\to ggg}^{\rm tree}}-\bigg[C_{A}\left(\alpha^{(1)}(-t_{1},\mu^{2})\log\frac{s_{45}}{\tau}+\alpha^{(1)}(-t_{2},\mu^{2})\log\frac{s_{34}}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle+c_{g}^{(1)}(-t_{1},\tau,\mu^{2})+c_{g}^{(1)}(-t_{2},\tau,\mu^{2})-iC_{A}\frac{\pi}{4}\left(\alpha^{(1)}(-t_{1},\mu^{2})+\alpha^{(1)}(-t_{2},\mu^{2})\right)\bigg].

(98)

The signaturised amplitude
\mathcal{M}^{(-,-),(1)}_{gg\to ggg}
is the colour projection of the five gluon scattering amplitude
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{gg\to ggg}
onto the component
c^{[8_{a},8_{a}]}
of the orthonormal basis in appendix
B
. To compute the amplitude we used the results of ref.
[
64
]

for the five-gluon amplitudes, which are given as
a decomposition in terms of one-loop master integrals
valid to all orders in
\epsilon
. They are written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{gg\to ggg}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{5}}\sum_{i=1}^{11}R_{\sigma,i}\,I_{\sigma,i},

(99)

where
\sigma
is a permutation of the 5 external gluons and the
I_{\sigma,i}
are a basis of one-loop master integrals.
This is a particular case of the type of decompositions
we discussed in section
3
,
and these expressions can be expanded in the MRK
limit with the techniques described there (see in
particular the comments at the end of sections
3.1

and
3.2
).
All the master integrals have been written in terms of powers of logarithms of
s_{34}>0
,
s_{45}>0
,
|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}>0
, and Goncharov multiple polylogarithms (GPLs)
[
101
,
102
]
with the symbol alphabet

\displaystyle\{z,\bar{z},1-z,1-\bar{z},z-{\bar{z}},1-z-{\bar{z}}\},

(100)

where we recall the relations in eq. (
8
) in the physical region, which to leading power in the MRK limit read

-t_{1}\simeq(1-z)(1-\bar{z})|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}>0\qquad\text{and}\qquad-t_{2}\simeq z\bar{z}|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}>0\,,

so
z
and
\bar{z}
are complex conjugate.
Notably, the latter transcendental functions appear only in single-valued combinations, and we used the Maple code
HyperlogProcedures

[
103
]
to write all the master integrals, up to transcendental weight 6, in terms of Single Valued Multiple Polylogarithms (SVMPLs)
[
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
,
109
,
79
,
20
,
21
,
103
]
.
Note that the notation used for SVMPLs (
{\cal I}
) in the
HyperlogProcedures
code, relates to the one we use here (
{\cal{G}}
) via

{\cal{G}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},z)\equiv{\cal I}(z,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},0)\,

(101)

where the heading
z
and the trailing
0
represent the limits of the final (single-valued) integration.
By plugging these results for the master integrals into eq. (
99
), we obtained the amplitude
\mathcal{M}^{(-,-),(1)}
up to
{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{4})
and, via eq. (
95
), the one-loop Lipatov vertex
v^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\tau,\mu^{2})
to the same order in
\epsilon
.
We provide a replacement list for the single-valued functions
{\cal G}
into ordinary GPLs,
G
, which may evaluated numerically using the GiNaC library
[
110
]
via PolyLogTools
[
79
]
.

z
\bar{z}
z=0
z=1
\bar{z}=1
\bar{z}=0
z=\bar{z}
z+\bar{z}=1

Figure 2
:
Lines in the
z
and
\bar{z}
plane identifying the position of physical (
z=0,1
and
\bar{z}=0,1
) and spurious (
z=\bar{z}
and
z+\bar{z}=1
) singularities of the Lipatov vertex (or the amplitude at MRK). The physical singularities (drawn in blue) represent the zeros of the squared transverse momentum
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
of (
8
) carried by the Reggeized gluons (additional singularities appear when these invariant diverge, at
z\to\infty
and
\bar{z}\to\infty
). The spurious singularity at
z=\bar{z}
(in red) represents, at the level of the amplitude, the limit where
{\rm tr}_{5}\equiv-4\,i\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\sigma\rho}P_{1}^{\mu}P_{2}^{\nu}P_{3}^{\sigma}P_{5}^{\rho}=0
, as can be seen from eq. (
9
). Finally, spurious singularity at
z+\bar{z}=1
(in olive green) corresponds to the soft limit where
|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}\to 0
and
t_{1}=t_{2}\equiv t
.

The dependence of
v^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\tau,\mu^{2})
on the factorisation and the renormalisation scales can be described to all-orders in
\epsilon
by

\displaystyle v^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\tau,\mu^{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}\,v^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2})+\frac{b_{0}}{8\epsilon}\left[\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{|\mathbf{p_{4}}|^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}-1\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha_{g}^{(1)}C_{A}}{2}\log\left(\frac{|\mathbf{p_{4}}|^{2}}{\tau}\right)\left[\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t_{1}}\right)^{\epsilon}+\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t_{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}\right]\,.

(102)

The procedure of obtaining eq. (
102
) is described in appendix
D
, and it can by straightforwardly extended to higher-loop orders.
Using appendix
D
and eq. (
102
) we have defined

v^{(1)}(z,\bar{z})\equiv v^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2})\equiv v^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\tau=|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\mu^{2}=|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}).

(103)

This single complex function captures the complexity of the one-loop Lipatov vertex, due to its
dependence on the three transverse momenta
\mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{q}_{2}
and
\mathbf{p}_{4}
.
v^{(1)}(z,\bar{z})
is an analytic function of the complex conjugate pair of variables
z
and
\bar{z}
, introduced in eq. (
10
). Figure
2
describes the location of singularities and potential singularities of the vertex as a function of
z
and
\bar{z}
.
The lines
z=0,1
and
\bar{z}=0,1
in the
(z,\bar{z})
plane mark the physical singularities of the amplitude. These lines correspond to the poles associated to the Reggeized gluon propagators with virtualities
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
going on shell. There are two more regions in the
(z,\bar{z})
plane associated to potential singularities of the amplitude. One is the line
z=\bar{z}
, where the invariant
\text{tr}_{5}
, defined in eq. (
3
), vanishes, following eq. (
9
).
This corresponds to the situation where all 5 parton 3-momenta are lying in a plane, or alternatively
that the three transverse momenta of the vertex are aligned, i.e. they admit

(\mathbf{q}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{2})^{2}-|\mathbf{q}_{1}|^{2}|\mathbf{q}_{2}|^{2}=\frac{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{4}(z-\bar{z})^{2}}{4}\to 0\,.

(104)

The second potential singularity is the line
1-z-\bar{z}=0
, corresponding to the soft limit,
|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}\to 0
, where all components of the emitted gluon momentum vanish and the two
t
channel invariants become equal,
t_{1}=t_{2}\equiv t
.

It is interesting to examine
the soft limit of the vertex in more detail.
In Figure
2
the line
z+\bar{z}=1
corresponds to this limit. We note that reflection about this line, eq. (
92
), correspond to target-projectile symmetry.
Because the
|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}\to 0
limit is taken keeping
t_{1}=-|\mathbf{q}_{1}|^{2}
and
t_{2}=-|\mathbf{q}_{2}|^{2}
finite, the variables

z
and
\bar{z}
develop in this limit large imaginary parts (see eq. (
8
)):

z(\bar{z})\to\frac{1+\delta}{2}\pm i\sqrt{\frac{|\mathbf{q}_{2}|^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}}-\frac{(1-\delta)^{2}}{4}}\,\,,\qquad\quad|\mathbf{p}_{4}|\to 0,\quad\delta=\frac{|\mathbf{q}_{1}|^{2}-|\mathbf{q}_{2}|^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}}\to 0\,.

(105)

In ref.
[
58
,
59
,
62
]
the sum and the difference of the right and left vertices
8
8

8

Equivalently, one may use
\widetilde{v}_{R}
and
\widetilde{v}_{L}
defined here in (
85
), since the expansion of the cosine functions in (
83
) does not contribute at one loop.
of eq. (
76
), have been computed to all orders in
\epsilon
.
Using these results in eqs. (
88a
) and (
88b
) respectively, we derive the soft limit of the dispersive and absorptive parts of the complex vertex
v_{\oplus}
at one loop (defined by eq. (
71
) for positive helicity) to all orders in
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}\to 0}\!v_{\oplus}^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2},\tau)=1+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2})}{\pi}\left\{-\frac{b_{0}}{8\epsilon}+\frac{N_{c}}{4}\frac{r_{\Gamma}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon}\left[2\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}\right)^{\epsilon}\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}}\right)\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.-\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\Psi(1-\epsilon)-\Psi(1+\epsilon)+\frac{i\pi}{2}\bigg)\right]\right\}\end{split}

(106)

where

\lim_{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}\to 0}t_{1}=\lim_{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}\to 0}t_{2}\equiv t\,,

and
r_{\Gamma}(\epsilon)
is defined in eq. (
64
).
This result in the soft limit (
106
) provides a useful check of our general kinematics calculation below.

We stress that while there are alphabet letters (
100
) which vanish on
z=\bar{z}
or on
z+\bar{z}=1
, there are
no physical singularities in these kinematic limits, only spurious poles. In fact, spurious poles only appear as
z+\bar{z}\to 1
, and the terms containing these poles are known to all orders in
\epsilon

[
57
,
99
,
62
]
and it is convenient to single them out as follows, and separate the one-loop vertex as follows

v_{\oplus}^{(1)}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})=v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})+v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})+v_{\beta}^{(1)}(\epsilon),

(107)

where we isolate the renormalisation term

v_{\beta}^{(1)}(\epsilon)=-\frac{11N_{c}-2n_{f}}{24\epsilon},

(108)

and where
v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}
reads

v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})=c_{a}\,v^{(1)}_{a}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})+c_{b}\,v^{(1)}_{b}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2}),

(109)

with

c_{a}=N_{c}\,(1-\epsilon)-n_{f},\qquad c_{b}=-(11-7\epsilon)\,N_{c}+2(1-\epsilon)\,n_{f}

(110)

and

\displaystyle v^{(1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=e^{\epsilon\,\gamma_{\text{E}}}\frac{\Gamma(\epsilon)\Gamma(1\epsilon)^{2}(|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(4-2\epsilon)\bar{z}(1-z)}\Bigg[-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\frac{(-t_{1})^{1-\epsilon}-(-t_{2})^{1-\epsilon}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})}

\displaystyle+\frac{|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}(1+z-\bar{z})}{2}\left(\frac{t_{1}t_{2}\,((-t_{1})^{-\epsilon}-(-t_{2})^{-\epsilon})}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{3}}-\epsilon\frac{(-t_{1})^{1-\epsilon}+(-t_{2})^{1-\epsilon}}{2(t_{1}-t_{2})^{2}}\right)\Bigg]

(111a)

\displaystyle v^{(1)}_{b}

\displaystyle=-e^{\epsilon\,\gamma_{\text{E}}}|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2}(1+z-\bar{z})\,\frac{\Gamma(\epsilon)\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\Gamma(2-\epsilon)(|\mathbf{p}_{4}|^{2})^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(4-2\epsilon)}\frac{(-t_{1})^{-\epsilon}-(-t_{2})^{-\epsilon}}{4\,(t_{1}-t_{2})}\,.

(111b)

The expansion in
\epsilon
of eq. (
111
) is straightforwards:
v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}
is finite as
\epsilon\to 0
and the only transcendental functions appearing are (single-valued) logarithms of
|z|^{2}
or of
|1-z|^{2}
.

Importantly, both
v^{(1)}_{a}
and
v^{(1)}_{b}
vanish in the soft limit, eq. (
105
), despite the denominators
t_{1}-t_{2}
in eq. (
111
).
This can be checked to all orders in
\epsilon
, by writing
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
in terms of
z
and
\bar{z}
and taking the (commuting) expansions around
|\mathbf{p}_{4}|\to 0
and
\delta\to 0
to find that both
v^{(1)}_{a}
and
v^{(1)}_{b}
are power suppressed.

We note that
v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}
does not contribute at maximal transcendental weight, hence it should not
[
111
,
112
]
contribute to the
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang Mills (sYM) vertex.
Indeed, by inspecting the limit
\epsilon\to 0
of the colour factors entering
v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}
, eq. (
110
), we recover the supersymmetric decomposition presented in Ref.
[
59
]

c_{a}\to N_{c}-n_{f},\qquad c_{b}\to-(11N_{c}-2n_{f}).

(112)

This shows that neither
c_{a}
or
c_{b}
can contribute to the sYM vertex: the former vanishes in the supersymmetric limit, while the latter is proportional to the running of the coupling, much like
v^{(1)}_{\beta}
, which vanishes for
{\cal N}=4
sYM.

The remaining contribution to the one-loop vertex in eq. (
107
)
is
v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})
, which can be identified as the Lipatov vertex in
\mathcal{N}=4
sYM. This contribution is free of spurious poles, and is expressed exclusively in terms of SVMPLs of maximal weight, in line with general expectations
[
111
,
112
]
. We extracted this function through
{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{4})
(weight 6).
We also present below the explicit expression for
v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}
through
{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{2})
, separated into its dispersive and absorptive components:

Disp.

\displaystyle\{v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}\}=-\frac{N_{c}}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{N_{c}}{8}\left[\frac{5\pi^{2}}{6}-\log^{2}\left(\frac{|z|^{2}}{|1-z|^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\epsilon\,N_{c}\,\Big[\frac{{\cal{G}}^{3}(0,z)+{\cal{G}}^{3}(1,z)}{12}-\frac{1}{4}\Big({\cal{G}}(0,0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(0,1,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(0,1,1,z)

\displaystyle+2{\cal{G}}(0,1-\bar{z},0,z)-2{\cal{G}}(0,1-\bar{z},1,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,1,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,1,0,z)\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi^{2}}{24}\,\log\left(|z|^{2}|1-z|^{2}\right)+\frac{13}{12}\,\zeta_{3}\Big]

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\,N_{c}\,\Big[\frac{1}{4}\Big({\cal{G}}(0,0,0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(0,0,1,0,z)-{\cal{G}}(0,1,0,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(0,0,1,1,z)

\displaystyle+2{\cal{G}}(0,0,1-\bar{z},0,z)-2{\cal{G}}(0,0,1-\bar{z},1,z)+{\cal{G}}(0,1,0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(0,1,1,0,z)

\displaystyle+{\cal{G}}(0,1,1,1,z)+2{\cal{G}}(0,1,1-\bar{z},0,z)-2{\cal{G}}(0,1,1-\bar{z},1,z)+2{\cal{G}}(0,1-\bar{z},0,0,z)

\displaystyle-2{\cal{G}}(0,1-\bar{z},1,1,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,0,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(1,0,1,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,1,1,z)

\displaystyle+2{\cal{G}}(1,0,1-\bar{z},0,z)-2{\cal{G}}(1,0,1-\bar{z},1,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,1,0,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,1,0,1,z)

\displaystyle+{\cal{G}}(1,1,1,0,z),+2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},0,1,0,z)-2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},0,1-\bar{z},0,z)+2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},0,1-\bar{z},1,z)

\displaystyle-2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},1,0,1,z)-2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},1,1-\bar{z},0,z)+2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},1,1-\bar{z},1,z)\Big)-\frac{{\cal{G}}^{4}(0,z)+{\cal{G}}^{4}(1,z)}{32}

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi^{2}}{96}\Big({\cal{G}}^{4}(0,z)+{\cal{G}}^{2}(1,z)+2(9{\cal{G}}(0,1,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,z)-8{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},1,z))\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{\zeta_{3}}{4}({\cal{G}}(0,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,z))+\frac{13\pi^{4}}{1920}\Big],

(113a)

Absorp.

\displaystyle\{v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}\}=\frac{\pi N_{c}}{8}\Big[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\epsilon\left({\cal{G}}(0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(1,0,z)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\right)-\epsilon^{2}\Big({\cal{G}}(0,0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(0,1,0,z)

\displaystyle+{\cal{G}}(0,1,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(1,0,0,z)+{\cal{G}}(1,0,1,z)-{\cal{G}}(1,1,0,z)-2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},0,1,z)

\displaystyle+2{\cal{G}}(\bar{z},1,0,z)-\frac{7\zeta_{3}}{3}\Big)\Big],

(113b)

where the functions
{\cal{G}}
are SVMPLs, which can be converted to multi-valued Goncharov multiple polylogarithms using the package
HyperlogProcedures

[
103
]

(recall the relation between notations in (
101
)).
In the ancillary files
[
70
]
, we provide both expressions of
v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}
up to
{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{4})
. The expression written in terms of SVMPL is very compact. Writing it in terms of these functions manifests the absence of branch points in the physical region, where
z
and
\bar{z}
are complex conjugates.

In order to compare with the literature
[
99
,
62
]
it is useful to relate the dispersive (absorptive) part of the vertex to the sum (difference) of the left and right vertices
\widetilde{v}_{L}
and
\widetilde{v}_{R}
using eq. (
88a
) and (
88b
), respectively.
These, we recall, are related through contraction with the polarization vector

{v}_{R}\pm v_{L}=\varepsilon^{\mu}_{\oplus}(P_{4})\,(R_{\mu}\pm L_{\mu})
to the quantities
R^{\mu}\pm L^{\mu}
that were determined in Ref.
[
62
]
through
{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{2})
, in terms of polylogarithmic functions and of
\mathcal{M}
-functions
[
60
]
. The latter arise in one of the master integrals computed in Refs.
[
99
,
62
]
, named
{\cal{I}}_{5}-{\cal{L}}_{3}
, which involves the 4-dimensional scalar pentagon integral. To better compare with our results in eqs. (
113a
) and (
113b
), we derived a different representation of the 4-dimensional pentagon integral in terms of the master integrals
I_{\sigma,i}
of eq. (
99
).
We find that
R^{\mu}+L^{\mu}
contains spurious poles at
t_{1}=t_{2}
, which match
v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}
of eq. (
110
). The remaining terms in
R^{\mu}+L^{\mu}
and
R^{\mu}-L^{\mu}
agree with eqs. (
113a
) and (
113b
), respectively.

It is interesting to see how the two properties we discussed in section
4.2
, reality and target-projectile symmetry, are realised in the one loop vertex.
We have seen that the reality property is only expected to hold prior to contraction with the polarization vector, so while the transcendental functions can be chosen hermitian (
94
), the rational coefficients would not be so.
In turn, target-projectile symmetry holds for the complex vertex, and the expectation is that if the transcendental functions are chosen to be invariant under

\{z\to 1-\bar{z},\,\,\,\bar{z}\to 1-{z}\}.

(114)

so will be their rational coefficients.

Let’s now examine the results for the
one-loop vertex. Consider first the contribution
v^{(1)}_{\text{spurious}}
. We see immediately that this term is real: it trivially obeys
z\leftrightarrow\bar{z}
symmetry, since it depends only on
t_{i}
of (
8
). We also observe that this contribution is manifestly symmetric under the target-projectile symmetry (
114
): in eq. (
111
) both the rational prefactors and each of the terms depending on
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
separately have this symmetry.

Consider next
v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}(t_{1},t_{2},|p_{4}|^{2})
. Also here our expectations hold, but the way this is realised is more interesting, as the symbol alphabet (
100
) transforms non-trivially under both
z\leftrightarrow\bar{z}
and under
z\leftrightarrow 1-\bar{z}
.
While
\text{Disp.}\{v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}\}
and
\text{Absorp.}\{v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}\}

separately satisfy the target-projectile symmetry (
114
), they transform nontrivally under
z\leftrightarrow\bar{z}
, as indeed expected, and they both have a an even (symmetric) as well as an odd (antisymmetric) components under this transformation.

We note in passing that while both the even and odd components of the dispersive part
of
v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}(z,\bar{z})
, as well as
the odd component of the absorptive part of
v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM}}(z,\bar{z})
, are all non-trivial (uniform weight) single-valued GPLs functions of the alphabet in eq. (
100
),
\,\text{Absorp.}\left\{v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM.Even}}(z,\bar{z})\right\}
is merely a uniform weight transcendental constant at any given order in
\epsilon
, consisting of both even and odd
\zeta
values. We obtain:

\text{Absorp.}\left\{v^{(1)}_{\text{sYM.Even}}(z,\bar{z})\right\}=\frac{\pi^{3}}{96}\epsilon+\frac{7\pi}{24}\zeta_{3}\epsilon^{2}+\frac{47\pi^{5}}{11520}\epsilon^{3}+\left(-\frac{7\pi^{3}}{288}\zeta_{3}+\frac{31\pi}{40}\zeta_{5}\right)\epsilon^{4}\,+\cdots\,.

(115)

6
The theory of multi-Reggeon interactions

As discussed in section
4

and
5
, up to NLL accuracy
the
(-,-)
component of the amplitude is
entirely captured by the exchange of a single
Reggeon. Beyond this logarithmic accuracy for

{\cal M}^{(-,-)}
, and in general for the
other signature components of the amplitude,
a correct description requires to take into
account the exchange of multiple Reggeons.
A framework to calculate their contribution
efficiently has been developed in
Refs.
[
17
,
18
,
113
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
.
The method is based on an effective-theory
approach, in which rapidity evolution equations
in the shockwave formalism are used to determine
all multi-Reggeon contributions to the amplitude.
Subsequently, by matching to the high-energy
expansion of the full amplitude, it is possible
to extract matching coefficients which are not
determined by the rapidity evolution, namely
the impact factors, the Regge trajectory and
(starting with
2\to 3
amplitudes) the
Lipatov vertex. In this section we review the
formalism and extend its application to the
calculation of
2\to 3
amplitudes, building
on earlier work in Ref.
[
17
,
21
]
.

6.1
From the shock-wave formalism to Reggeon fields

In the shockwave formalism
[
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
17
,
18
,
23
]

one describes the projectile as a product
of (an indefinite number of) Wilson-line
operators,
U({z_{1}}_{\perp})U({z_{2}}_{\perp})\ldots
,
each of which extends over the infinite
+

lightcone direction and located as a distinct
transverse position
z_{\perp}
:

U(z_{\perp})={\cal P}\,\exp\left[\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}T^{a}A_{+}^{a}(x^{+},x^{-}=0,z_{\perp})dx^{+}\right]\,.

(116)

The Wilson lines (
116
) have
rapidity divergences, so the operators

U({z_{1}}_{\perp})U({z_{2}}_{\perp})\ldots

admit rapidity evolution equations

-\frac{d}{d\eta}\left[U({z_{1}}_{\perp})U({z_{2}}_{\perp})\ldots\right]=H\left[U({z_{1}}_{\perp})U({z_{2}}_{\perp})\ldots\right]\,,

(117)

known as the Balitsky-JIMWLK
equations
[
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
,
where the leading-order Hamiltonian
is given by

H=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2\pi^{2}}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int[d{z}_{0}]\frac{{z}_{0i}\cdot{z}_{0j}}{\big({z}_{0i}^{2}{z}_{0j}^{2}\big)^{1-\epsilon}}\left(T_{i,L}^{a}T_{j,L}^{a}+T_{i,R}^{a}T_{j,R}^{a}-U_{\rm adj}^{ab}({z}_{0})(T_{i,L}^{a}T_{j,R}^{b}+T_{j,L}^{a}T_{i,R}^{b})\right)\,,

(118)

where
z_{0i}\equiv z_{0}-z_{i}
and
[dz]\equiv d^{2{-}2\epsilon}z
.
Here we work exclusively in the
2-2\epsilon
dimensional
transverse plane, and therefore we drop the boldface
notation we used earlier for the transverse components.
In eq. (
118
) we used functional derivative
operators,
T_{i,L}^{a}
and
T_{i,R}^{a}
, which generate
colour rotation. They are defined by

T_{i,L}^{a}\equiv T^{a}U({z}_{i})\frac{\delta}{\delta U({z}_{i})},\qquad T_{i,R}^{a}\equiv U({z}_{i})T^{a}\frac{\delta}{\delta U({z}_{i})}\,.

(119)

The action of the Hamiltonian
H
on the product
of Wilson lines involves an additional Wilson line
in the adjoint representation
U_{\rm adj}^{ab}({z}_{0})

generated through the interaction with the target
shockwave. Thus this system of equations is
non-linear and each iteration of the evolution
involves an increasing number of Wilson lines.
Clearly it is very hard to solve this system
in general. However, in the perturbative regime
where fields are weak, each Wilson
U(z_{\perp})

is close to unity. The dynamics is then best
described in terms of its logarithm
W
, defined
via
[
17
]

U(z_{\perp})=e^{ig_{s}T^{a}W^{a}(z_{\perp})}\,.

(120)

The field
W
can be identified as sourcing a
single Reggeon exchanged in the
t
channel.
Wilson lines
U
(and products thereof) can
be thus described perturbatively, order by
order in
g_{s}
as an expansion in
W
fields,
all at the same transverse position,

\displaystyle U=e^{ig_{s}\,W^{a}T^{a}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+ig_{s}\,W^{a}\,T^{a}-\frac{g_{s}^{2}}{2}W^{a}W^{b}\,T^{a}T^{b}-i\frac{g^{3}_{s}}{6}W^{a}W^{b}W^{c}\,T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}

(121)

\displaystyle+\,\frac{g^{4}_{s}}{24}W^{a}W^{b}W^{c}W^{d}\,T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}T^{d}+{\cal O}(g_{s}^{5}\,W^{5}),

where
n
fields
W
source
n

Reggeons
[
17
]
.
We can also express the action of
the colour rotation operators

iT_{j,L/R}^{a}
defined in
(
119
) in terms of
functional derivatives of
W

\displaystyle iT_{j,L/R}^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{g_{s}}\frac{\delta}{\delta W_{j}^{a}}\pm\frac{1}{2}f^{abx}W_{j}^{x}\frac{\delta}{\delta W_{j}^{b}}-\frac{g_{s}}{12}W_{j}^{x}W_{j}^{y}(F^{x}F^{y})^{a}{}_{b}\frac{\delta}{\delta W_{j}^{b}}

(122)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\,\frac{g_{s}^{3}}{720}W_{j}^{x}W_{j}^{y}W_{j}^{z}W_{j}^{t}(F^{x}F^{y}F^{z}F^{t})^{a}{}_{b}\frac{\delta}{\delta W_{j}^{b}}+\ldots,

where we call
W_{j}\equiv W(z_{j})
.
In eq. (
122
) we have introduced
the Hermitian colour matrix

(F^{x})^{a}{}_{b}\equiv if^{axb}

for simplicity.

Through this expansion,
Refs.
[
18
,
23
]

have translated the action of the Balitsky-JIMWLK
Hamiltonian in eq. (
117
) to an
action on a vector of increasing order in the
number of
W
fields, which schematically
takes the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}H\left(\begin{array}[]{c}W\\
WW\\
WWW\\
\cdots\end{array}\right)&\equiv&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}H_{1{\to}1}&0&H_{3{\to}1}&\ldots\\
0&H_{2{\to}2}&0&\ldots\\
H_{1{\to}3}&0&H_{3{\to}3}&\ldots\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}W\\
WW\\
WWW\\
\cdots\end{array}\right)\\
&\sim&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}g_{s}^{2}&0&g_{s}^{4}&\ldots\\
0&g_{s}^{2}&0&\ldots\\
g_{s}^{4}&0&g_{s}^{2}&\ldots\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}W\\
WW\\
WWW\\
\cdots\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(123)

where the first line defines the components
of the Hamiltonian
H_{k\to m}
mediating
between a state with
k
Reggeons and one
with
m
Reggeons (note that transitions
between odd and even number of Reggeons
are forbidden) while the second line
indicates the order in
g_{s}
at which
each such component of the Hamiltonian
begins. In this way the perturbative
expansion of the Wilson lines in effect
linearises the non-linear evolution, and
upon working to a given order in the
coupling, the system involves a restricted
number of Reggeons.

In
2\to 2
scattering
ij\to i^{\prime}j^{\prime}
one
defines (see
[
23
]
for
more details
9
9

9

Notice that we
divide by the unobservable phase
associated with the tree-level impact
factors
C_{i/j}^{(0)}
, see e.g.
eq. (
323
). The removal
of this phase is taken into account
also in
[
23
]
,
although not explicitly stated.
):

\displaystyle\big|\psi_{i}\big\rangle\equiv\frac{\Big(Z_{i}C_{i}^{(0)}\Big)^{-1}}{2p_{i}^{+}}a_{i^{\prime}}(p_{i^{\prime}})a^{{\dagger}}_{i}(p_{i})|0\rangle,\qquad\big\langle\psi_{j}\big|\equiv\frac{\Big(Z_{j}C_{j}^{(0)}\Big)^{-1}}{2p_{j}^{-}}\langle 0|a_{j^{\prime}}(p_{j^{\prime}})a^{{\dagger}}_{j}(p_{j}),

(124)

where
a^{{\dagger}}_{i}
and
a_{i^{\prime}}
are
respectively creation and annihilation
operators associated with the projectile,
while
a^{{\dagger}}_{j}
and
a_{j^{\prime}}
are
associated with the target. Next,
modelling these states in terms of
products of (an indefinite number of)
Wilson lines
U
, one may represent them
as an expansion in terms of states with a
definite number
n
of Reggeons
W
,

\displaystyle\big|\psi_{i}\big\rangle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\ket{\psi_{i,n}},\qquad\quad\big\langle\psi_{j}\big|=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bra{\psi_{j,n}}.

(125)

An
n
Reggeon state
\big|\psi_{i}\big\rangle
can in turn be expanded

\ket{\psi_{i,n}}=\ket{\psi_{i,n}}^{\text{LO}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\ket{\psi_{i,n}}^{\text{N}^{k}\text{LO}}\,.

(126)

Here leading order (LO) contributions
arise from the expansion of a single
Wilson line
U(z_{\perp})
, in which
case

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,1}}^{\text{LO}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ig_{s}\,\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\,W^{a}(p)|0\rangle,

(127a)

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,2}}^{\text{LO}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g_{s}^{2}}{2}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{b}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq]\,W^{a}(q)W^{b}(p{-}q)|0\rangle,

(127b)

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,3}}^{\text{LO}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{ig_{s}^{3}}{6}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{b}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{c}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq_{1}][d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq_{2}]\,W^{a}(q_{1})W^{b}(q_{2})W^{c}(p{-}q_{1}{-}q_{2})|0\rangle,

(127c)

where we use the following convention
for the Fourier transform of the
W
fields:

W^{a}(p)=\int[dz]\,e^{-ipz}\,W^{a}(z),\qquad W^{a}(z)=\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptp]\,e^{ipz}\,W^{a}(p).

(128)

and the measure is

[dz]\equiv d^{2{-}2\epsilon}z,\qquad\quad[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptp]\equiv\frac{d^{2-2\epsilon}p}{(2\pi)^{2-2\epsilon}}.

(129)

At next-to-leading order (NLO) two Wilson
lines at distinct transverse positions play
a role, and then non-trivial impact factors
for the emission of a given number of Reggeons
arise:

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,1}}^{\text{NLO}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ig_{s}\,\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}d_{i}^{(1)}(-p^{2})\,W^{a}(p)|0\rangle,

(130a)

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,2}}^{\text{NLO}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g_{s}^{2}}{2}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\mathbf{T}_{i}^{b}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq]\,\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\psi^{(1)}_{i}(p,q)\,W^{a}(q)W^{b}(p{-}q)|0\rangle\,.

(130b)

Similarly, at next-to-next-to-leading order (NNLO) one has

\ket{\psi_{i,1}}^{\text{NNLO}}=ig_{s}\,\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\,\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{2}d_{i}^{(2)}(-p^{2},\tau)\,W^{a}(p)|0\rangle,

(131)

and so on. In particular, the all orders
generalization of the 1-Reggeon wavefunction
reads

\ket{\psi_{i,1}}=ig_{s}\,\mathbf{T}_{i}^{a}\,d_{i}(-p^{2},\tau)\,W^{a}(p)|0\rangle,

(132)

where the impact factor
d_{i}(-p^{2},\tau)

has perturbative expansion

d_{i}(-p^{2},\tau)=1+\sum_{n}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{n}d_{i}^{(n)}(-p^{2},\tau)\,.

(133)

Comparing with eqs. (
66
),
(
67
) and (
124
),
it is easy to see that the functions
d_{i}

represent the perturbative correction to the
impact factors with collinear singularities
removed:

C^{\rm SR}_{i}(p_{i},p_{i^{\prime}},\tau)=C^{(0)}_{i}(p_{i},p_{i^{\prime}})\,Z_{i}(t_{i},\tau)\,d_{i}(t_{i},\tau)\,,

(134)

or, equivalently,

c^{\rm SR}_{i}(t_{i},\tau)=Z_{i}(t_{i},\tau)\,d_{i}(t_{i},\tau)\,.

(135)

Notice that in eqs. (
134
) and (
135
)
and in what follows we add a superscript “SR”, for “single
Reggeon”, in order to stress that the perturbative correction
to the impact factors are defined as matching coefficients that arise naturally
within the multi-Reggeon effective theory, identifying a Reggeon with a
W
field. To be precise, they are defined such that any contribution of the amplitude associated exclusively with a single
W
field, throughout the entire rapidity span between the target and the projectile, is considered a SR transition, while all other contributions to the amplitude in which more than one
W
field is involved in the transition (at any stage, even over part of the rapidity span or through mixing under evolution), is considered a MR transition. Matching in the SR/MR scheme means factorizing only the SR component of the amplitude when defining the impact factors, the Regge trajectory and the Reggeon-gluon-Reggeon vertex, while excluding all MR transitions.
The alternative, pole/cut scheme originally defined
in
[
24
,
23
]
will be discussed
in section
8
.

Having expressed the projectile and the
target in terms of states consisting of
a definite number of Reggeons, and
additionally knowing how the Hamitonian acts
on these states (see (
123
)) to
generate rapidity evolution, we can determine
how the projectile (and likewise the target)
evolves, namely

-\frac{d}{d\eta}\big|\psi_{i}\big\rangle=H\big|\psi_{i}\big\rangle

(136)

With this we can compute
2\to 2
amplitudes
in the high-energy limit via

\frac{i}{2s}{\cal\bar{M}}_{ij\to ij}\equiv\frac{i\big(Z_{i}Z_{j}C_{i}^{(0)}C_{j}^{(0)}\big)^{-1}}{2s}{\cal M}_{ij\to ij}\equiv\braket{\psi_{j}|e^{-H\eta}|\psi_{i}}\,

(137)

where the factor
e^{-H\eta}
corresponds to
the evolution of the target to the rapidity of
the projectile (or vice versa) where
\eta
is
logarithm of the energy ratio. This technique was
developed and used in Ref.
[
18
]

to compute the NNLL tower of logarithms of

2\to 2
amplitudes to three loops and in
Refs.
[
22
,
23
,
24
]

to four loops.

6.2
Real emission from Reggeons

In eq. (
137
) we expressed
2\to 2
high-energy amplitude through a rapidity evolution of target and projectile states that can be expanded in terms of a fixed number of Reggeons.
In a similar way, one writes the
2\to 3

amplitude
i(p_{1})j(p_{2})\to i^{\prime}(p_{5})g(p_{4})j^{\prime}(p_{3})

in MRK as follows
[
17
,
18
,
21
]
:

\frac{i}{2s_{12}}{\cal\bar{M}}_{ij\to i^{\prime}gj^{\prime}}\equiv\frac{i\big(Z_{i}Z_{j}C_{i}^{(0)}C_{j}^{(0)}\big)^{-1}}{2s_{12}}{\cal M}_{ij\to i^{\prime}gj^{\prime}}=\big\langle\psi_{j}\big|e^{-H\eta_{2}}\,a_{4}(p_{4})\,e^{-H\eta_{1}}\big|\psi_{i}\big\rangle,

(138)

where
\eta_{1,2}\gg 1
represents the large
energy logarithms. These can be chosen to
coincide with the logarithms in eq. (
72
),
namely

\eta_{1}=\log\left(\frac{s_{45}}{\tau}\right)-i\frac{\pi}{4}\,,\quad\qquad\eta_{2}=\log\left(\frac{s_{34}}{\tau}\right)-i\frac{\pi}{4}\,,

(139)

The new element of eq. (
138
)
compared to the case of
2\to 2
scattering
is the presence of the annihilation operator
for the gluon
a_{4}(p_{4})
. The first evolution
operator
e^{-H\eta_{1}}
evolves the Reggeons
appearing in the expansion of
|\psi_{i}\rangle

to the rapidity of the produced gluon with
momentum
p_{4}
(similarly,
e^{-H\eta_{2}}

evolves the Reggeons appearing in
|\psi_{j}\rangle

to the rapidity of the gluon
p_{4}
). Therefore,
we need to determine the action of the operator

a_{4}(p_{4})
on a set of Wilson lines
U(z_{i})
,
and the corresponding linearised action of

a_{4}(p_{4})
on Reggeon fields
W
. This
has been determined in
[
17
]
,
and we review the derivation in what follows
before proceeding to use it in evaluating
(
138
).

First of all, we need the action of the
operator
a_{4}
on a Wilson line
U(z_{1})
,
which reads
[
17
]

U(z_{1})\,a^{a}(p_{2})=2g_{s}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq][dz_{2}]\frac{q\cdot\varepsilon(p_{2})}{q^{2}}e^{iq\cdot(z_{2}-z_{1})}e^{-ip_{2}\cdot z_{2}}\,\Big(U^{ab}_{\rm ad}(z_{2})T_{1,R}^{b}-T_{1,L}^{a}\Big)U(z_{1}),

(140)

where the polarization vector

\varepsilon(p_{2})\equiv\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(p_{2})

refers to the transverse component of the
full gluon polarization vector
10
10

10

Taking
into account our convention for Fourier
transformation in eq. (
128
),
eq. (
140
) is equivalent to the definition
given in eq. (5.7) of
[
17
]
.
We check in section
6.3
that starting
from eq. (
140
) we reproduce the correct tree level
amplitude.
. Recall that

U^{ab}_{\rm ad}(z_{2})
is a Wilson line in
the adjoint representation at position
z_{2}
,
where the gluon crosses the shockwave, and
the colour rotations
T^{a}_{R/L,i}
are defined
in eq. (
119
). Using eqs. (
121
)
and (
122
) to expand

U^{ab}_{\rm ad}(z_{2})
and
T^{a}_{R/L,i}

we obtain the master equation

\displaystyle U(z_{1})\,a^{a}(p_{2})\sim 2i\,g_{s}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq][dz_{2}]\frac{q\cdot\varepsilon(p_{2})}{q^{2}}e^{iq\cdot(z_{2}-z_{1})}e^{-ip_{2}\cdot z_{2}}\bigg\{i(F^{c})^{a}{}_{b}\Big[W_{1}^{c}-W_{2}^{c}\Big]

\displaystyle-\,\frac{g_{s}}{2}(F^{cd})^{a}{}_{b}\Big[W_{2}^{c}\Big(W_{1}^{d}-W_{2}^{d}\Big)\Big]+\,\frac{g_{s}^{2}}{12}i(F^{cde})^{a}{}_{b}\Big[W_{2}^{c}W_{1}^{d}W_{1}^{e}-3W_{2}^{c}W_{2}^{d}W_{1}^{e}+2W_{2}^{c}W_{2}^{d}W_{2}^{e}\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{g_{s}^{3}}{24}(F^{cdef})^{a}{}_{b}\Big[W_{2}^{c}W_{2}^{d}W_{1}^{e}W_{1}^{f}-2W_{2}^{c}W_{2}^{d}W_{2}^{e}W_{1}^{f}+W_{2}^{c}W_{2}^{d}W_{2}^{e}W_{2}^{f}\Big]\bigg\}\frac{\delta}{\delta W_{1}^{b}}U(z_{1})\,.

(141)

where we included in the expansion up to
terms of
{\cal O}({W^{4}})
, using the shorthand
notation for
W_{i}^{c}=W^{a}(z_{i})
and
F^{a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}}\equiv F^{a_{1}}F^{a_{2}}\dots F^{a_{n}}
. Eq. (
140
)
determines the action of the operator
a^{a}(p_{2})
on
an arbitrary number of Reggeon fields. The
corresponding vertices are obtained by
expanding the Wilson line
U(z_{1})
up
to the required number of Reggeons.

First, we consider the action of
a^{a}(p_{2})

on a single Reggeon, by expanding
U(z_{1})
to
the linear order in
W^{a}(z_{1})
and then taking
the Fourier transform according to
eq. (
128
). We get

\displaystyle W^{a}(p_{1})\,a^{b}(p_{2})

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\bigg\{-2i\,g_{s}\,f^{abc}W^{c}(p_{1}+p_{2})\bigg[\frac{p^{\mu}_{1}}{p_{1}^{2}}+\frac{p_{2}^{\mu}}{p_{2}^{2}}\bigg]

(142)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-\,i\,g^{2}_{s}\,f^{bce}f^{ead}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptk]\,W^{c}(p_{1}+p_{2}-k)W^{d}(k)\bigg[\frac{p_{1}^{\mu}}{p_{1}^{2}}-\frac{(p_{1}-k)^{\mu}}{(p_{1}-k)^{2}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+\,i\,\frac{g^{3}_{s}}{6}\,f^{bcx}f^{xdy}f^{yae}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptk_{1}][d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptk_{2}]\,W^{c}(p_{1}+p_{2}-k_{1}-k_{2})W^{d}(k_{1})W^{e}(k_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\,\bigg[\frac{(p_{1}-k_{1}-k_{2})^{\mu}}{(p_{1}-k_{1}-k_{2})^{2}}-3\frac{(p_{1}-k_{2})^{\mu}}{(p_{1}-k_{2})^{2}}+2\frac{p_{1}^{\mu}}{p_{1}^{2}}\bigg]\bigg\}\,\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{2})+\ldots,

These contributions are described by
figure
3
.
The terms of higher order, which are
omitted here, involve the coupling
between a gluon and five or more
Reggeons. These vertices start to
contribute to N
3
LL
and therefore are beyond the scope
of the present paper.

Figure 3
:
Diagrams describing the three vertices
as one Reggeon into one, two or three Reggeons
plus an emitted real gluon.

Next, we consider the term containing
two
W
fields from the expansion of
U(z_{1})
.
At leading order we obtain

\displaystyle\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq]W^{a}(q)W^{b}(p_{1}-q)\,a^{c}(p_{2})

\displaystyle\sim

(143)

\displaystyle\hskip-145.10922pt-2i\,g_{s}\int[d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptq]\Big\{f^{acd}\,W^{d}(p_{2}+q)W^{b}(p_{1}-q)\bigg[\frac{p_{2}^{\mu}}{p_{2}^{2}}+\frac{q^{\mu}}{q^{2}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip-93.89418pt+\,f^{bcd}\,W^{a}(q)W^{d}(p_{1}+p_{2}-q)\bigg[\frac{p_{2}^{\mu}}{p_{2}^{2}}+\frac{(p_{1}-q)^{\mu}}{(p_{1}-q)^{2}}\bigg]\bigg\}\varepsilon_{\mu}(p_{2})+\ldots,

where the ellipses stand for
WW\to WWW

and
WW\to WWWW
transitions not yet calculated,
which will enter only at N
3
LL.
The two-to-two Reggeon vertex is shown in
figure

.

Figure 4
:
A Diagram describing the two-to-two Reggeon
vertex associated with the emission of a real gluon.

The last contribution necessary to achieve
NNLL accuracy is given by the exchange of

which originates from considering the

W
fields from
the expansion of
U(z_{1})
. At leading
